What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because at work I keep getting up from my workstation during the day. I don’t always feel like I’m pacing or anything, but when I’m at my workstation, I keep ending up getting out of my seat. That’s the main thing that made me come in — I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay there and finish the claim reviews.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with things, like pens or papers, when you're sitting at your workstation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because at work I keep getting up from my workstation during the day. I know I’m supposed to stay there and finish reviewing claims, but I just keep leaving my seat, and it’s starting to be a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're sitting at your workstation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t always feel like I’m pacing or anything, but when I’m at my workstation, I keep ending up getting out of my seat. I know I’m supposed to stay there and get through the claim reviews, but I just keep leaving.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to the claim reviews while you're sitting at your workstation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If people near me start talking about another claim, or I suddenly remember some random errand I need to do, my attention just kind of slips off what I’m reading. Then I realize I didn’t take it in, and I have to reread the same section a few times.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting up or feeling restless at home or when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it at work. That’s the main thing that made me come in — I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay there and finish the claim reviews.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with things, like pens or papers, when you're sitting at your workstation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. In trainings or longer team meetings especially, I’m usually bouncing my leg or clicking my pen, or just shifting around in my chair even when I’m trying to look focused.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it wasn’t brand new. In elementary school, teachers would say I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, and need reminders to actually finish my classwork.\n\nDoctor: Are you having problems at work or in your relationships because of these difficulties with restlessness and paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, mainly at work. My supervisor put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind, and they’ve started questioning my accuracy. That’s been stressful because I was aiming for the senior adjuster track, and now I’m worried I could mess that up or even lose that chance.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I might jump in sometimes if I’m worried I’ll forget what I was going to say, but I don’t think I’m usually cutting people off or blurting answers before they finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, I can make quick choices sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I often do things without thinking about the consequences.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or belongings?\n\nPatient: Yes. Since my caseload got bigger, it’s been harder to keep everything straight. My claim folders, notes, and email flags kind of end up scattered, and then I’ll sit there trying to figure out which policy review I’m supposed to tackle first. It gets pretty disorganized.\n\nDoctor: Are you having problems in your personal relationships because of these difficulties with restlessness and paying attention?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed major relationship problems from it. Maybe people get a little frustrated if I seem distracted, but I can’t say it’s caused serious issues personally.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being especially restless compared to other kids. I was probably pretty normal activity-wise, as far as I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of time or forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think that happens to me in any big way. I might get wrapped up in something for a bit, but I haven’t really noticed losing track of time or everything around me like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress from the difficulties you're having with restlessness and paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it is causing me stress. In trainings or longer team meetings especially, I’m usually bouncing my leg or clicking my pen, or just shifting around in my chair even when I’m trying to look focused.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it wasn’t brand new. In elementary school, teachers would say I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, and need reminders to actually finish my classwork.\n\nDoctor: Are you having problems at work or in your relationships because of these difficulties with restlessness and paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, mainly at work. My supervisor put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind, and they’ve started questioning my accuracy. That’s been stressful because I was aiming for the senior adjuster track, and now I’m worried I could mess that up or even lose that chance.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I might jump in sometimes if I’m worried I’ll forget what I was going to say, but I don’t think I’m usually cutting people off or blurting answers before they finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, I can make quick choices sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I often do things without thinking about the consequences.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your tasks or belongings?\n\nPatient: Yes. Since my caseload got bigger, it’s been harder to keep everything straight. My claim folders, notes, and email flags kind of end up scattered, and then I’ll sit there trying to figure out which policy review I’m supposed to tackle first. It gets pretty disorganized.\n\nDoctor: Are you having problems in your personal relationships because of these difficulties with restlessness and paying attention?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed major relationship problems from it. Maybe people get a little frustrated if I seem distracted, but I can’t say it’s caused serious issues personally.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being especially restless compared to other kids. I was probably pretty normal activity-wise, as far as I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of time or forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think that happens to me in any big way. I might get wrapped up in something for a bit, but I haven’t really noticed losing track of time or everything around me like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress from the difficulties you're having with restlessness and paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it is causing me stress. At work my supervisor put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind, and they’re questioning my accuracy. I’m worried that if I don’t get it together, I could lose the chance to move into the senior adjuster track I was aiming for.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're at home or in other situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do notice it sometimes. Like in trainings or longer team meetings, I’ll be bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the chair, even when I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with restlessness and paying attention causing problems in your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused major problems with friends or family, at least nothing that stands out. Maybe I can be a little distracted sometimes, but I haven’t really had anyone make a big issue out of it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have symptoms like restlessness, fidgeting, or difficulty paying attention when you were in high school?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it wasn’t really brand new. Even in elementary school, teachers would write that I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, and need reminders to finish classwork. So I think the paying attention part was there pretty early on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Even in elementary school, teachers would write that I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, and need reminders to finish classwork. So I think the paying attention part was there pretty early on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed that happening a lot. Usually it’s more the opposite, where I’m trying to stay with something, but I don’t think I get so locked in that I tune everything else out.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no.
